Oh, well, I want to be clear that I’m definitely still getting all my work done! The attorneys I work for haven't complained or anything, I promise. But, um... I guess it has been on my mind a fair amount. 

I've been spending a little extra time getting ready in the mornings—just trying to smooth things out and do my makeup a bit differently, you know? And because of that, I admit I have had to rush into the courthouse a few mornings lately, which really isn't like me. And... well, I might have missed the very beginning of my yoga class more than once. I feel so silly even saying that out loud. I hope that doesn't sound incredibly vain?

I'm still doing the things I enjoy, mostly. But I suppose I have been avoiding the camera a little bit. Like at my book club or at these community events I help with, I’ll just kind of step out of the frame or offer to take the picture instead. I just don't want to spend the rest of the day worrying about how my face is going to look when they post the photos. I know it sounds shallow, I'm sorry. Is it normal to worry about things like that at my age?

Oh, um... distress is a strong word, isn't it? I wouldn't say it causes me a lot of distress. I mean, I'm certainly not sitting around crying about it or anything like that. I want to make sure you know I'm handling things okay overall. 

But I suppose... well, maybe sometimes I do think about it when I'm not in front of the mirror. Just a little bit. Like if I'm talking to one of the attorneys at work, or if I'm at the grocery store, I might wonder if the lighting is hitting my face a certain way, or if they're noticing the unevenness. It just kind of pops into my head, you know?

I try really hard to just push the thought away and focus on what I'm doing. It's not taking over my life or anything, I promise. I hope that's the kind of information you were looking for? I guess I've been focusing on it a lot, even though my husband says he can’t really see what I’m talking about at all. \n\nI know it's probably not a huge deal, and I'm sure other people have much bigger problems, but it's just been bothering me, and I thought I should maybe talk to someone. Does that make sense? I hope that's an okay reason to come in.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me a bit more about how much this concern with your cheeks and jawline has been on your mind lately? Has it been distracting you from work, relationships, or other things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Oh, well, I want to be clear that I’m definitely still getting all my work done! The attorneys I work for haven't complained or anything, I promise. But, um... I guess it has been on my mind a fair amount. \n\nI've been spending a little extra time getting ready in the mornings—just trying to smooth things out and do my makeup a bit differently, you know? And because of that, I admit I have had to rush into the courthouse a few mornings lately, which really isn't like me. And... well, I might have missed the very beginning of my yoga class more than once. I feel so silly even saying that out loud. I hope that doesn't sound incredibly vain?\n\nI'm still doing the things I enjoy, mostly. But I suppose I have been avoiding the camera a little bit. Like at my book club or at these community events I help with, I’ll just kind of step out of the frame or offer to take the picture instead. I just don't want to spend the rest of the day worrying about how my face is going to look when they post the photos. I know it sounds shallow, I'm sorry. Is it normal to worry about things like that at my age?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if this concern about your facial appearance ever causes you a lot of distress, even when you're not looking in the mirror or thinking about photos? And if so, how often does that happen?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... distress is a strong word, isn't it? I wouldn't say it causes me a lot of distress. I mean, I'm certainly not sitting around crying about it or anything like that. I want to make sure you know I'm handling things okay overall. \n\nBut I suppose... well, maybe sometimes I do think about it when I'm not in front of the mirror. Just a little bit. Like if I'm talking to one of the attorneys at work, or if I'm at the grocery store, I might wonder if the lighting is hitting my face a certain way, or if they're noticing the unevenness. It just kind of pops into my head, you know? \n\nI wouldn't say it happens constantly. Just... maybe a few times a day? Or maybe a little more on days when I'm feeling particularly tired. I try really hard to just push the thought away and focus on what I'm doing. It's not taking over my life or anything, I promise. I hope that's the kind of information you were looking for? I just don't want to make it sound worse than it really is.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you found yourself doing anything repeatedly—like checking mirrors, adjusting your appearance, or seeking reassurance—to try to cope with these thoughts about your face?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 46 / paralegal_or_legal_assistant / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors\nmanifestation: \"Most mornings I end up checking my face from a few angles and putting on a little extra makeup before I leave for the courthouse, and sometimes I compare my face to women I see at work or avoid being in casual pictures if I’m not feeling okay about it.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, well... I wouldn't say I do anything obsessively. I really don't want you to think I'm just standing around staring at myself all day! I know I have a job to do. 

But, um... most mornings, I suppose I do end up checking my face from a few different angles in that new mirror. Just to make sure everything looks okay. And I might put on just a little extra makeup before I leave for the courthouse, to try and smooth out the unevenness. I just want to look professional for the firm, you know? 

And... well, like I mentioned before, I do try to avoid being in casual pictures if I’m not feeling okay about how my jawline looks that day. And sometimes... I feel a little embarrassed even admitting this, but sometimes I catch myself comparing my face to the other women I see at work. Just looking to see if their skin is doing the same thing mine is. 

But I think most women my age probably do that a little bit, don't they? I really hope I'm not making it sound like I'm completely fixated on it, because I promise I'm not. I hope that answers your question alright?

Oh, upset? Um... I really wouldn't use words like that. I don't get really upset or anxious about it, I promise. I mean, I'm a grown woman, and I know there are much bigger problems in the world than a few wrinkles or some sagging skin, right? 

I guess... well, maybe there are fleeting moments where I feel a tiny bit frustrated if my makeup just isn't sitting right, or if the lighting in the office bathroom is particularly harsh. But it's just a passing thought, really. It doesn't last long at all. I just kind of sigh, fix my foundation a bit, and get right back to work. 

I definitely don't let it ruin my day or anything like that. I'm usually very positive! I hope that's what you were asking? I just really want to be clear that I'm not falling apart over this or anything.

You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, goodness, no. I really wouldn't say that at all. I mean... I'm a paralegal, so my job requires a lot of attention to detail, and I would never let something like this get in the way of my work. The attorneys rely on me too much, you know? 

So, no, it definitely doesn't make it hard to focus or interfere with my job. I promise I'm very dedicated to the firm. And as for enjoying my day... well, I still have a lovely time! Like I said, I still go to my yoga classes and my book club. I might have a... a brief moment where I wish my skin looked a bit firmer or more even, but I just push it right out of my mind and get back to what I'm doing. 

I really wouldn't let it consume my thoughts or ruin my day. I'm a very grounded person. I hope that reassures you? I just really don't want you to think I'm sitting at my desk obsessing over my face when I should be working. Does that answer your question okay?

I hope that doesn't sound incredibly vain?\n\nI'm still doing the things I enjoy, mostly. But I suppose I have been avoiding the camera a little bit. Like if I'm talking to one of the attorneys at work, or if I'm at the grocery store, I might wonder if the lighting is hitting my face a certain way, or if they're noticing the unevenness. It just kind of pops into my head, you know? \n\nI wouldn't say it happens constantly. Just... maybe a few times a day? Or maybe a little more on days when I'm feeling particularly tired. Oh, sad? No, no, I wouldn't say that. I really don't feel down or depressed at all. I'm a very upbeat person, honestly!

Is it normal to worry about things like that at my age?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if this concern about your facial appearance ever causes you a lot of distress, even when you're not looking in the mirror or thinking about photos? And if so, how often does that happen?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... distress is a strong word, isn't it? I wouldn't say it causes me a lot of distress. I mean, I'm certainly not sitting around crying about it or anything like that. I want to make sure you know I'm handling things okay overall. \n\nBut I suppose... well, maybe sometimes I do think about it when I'm not in front of the mirror. Just a little bit. Like if I'm talking to one of the attorneys at work, or if I'm at the grocery store, I might wonder if the lighting is hitting my face a certain way, or if they're noticing the unevenness. It just kind of pops into my head, you know? \n\nI wouldn't say it happens constantly. Just... maybe a few times a day? Or maybe a little more on days when I'm feeling particularly tired. I try really hard to just push the thought away and focus on what I'm doing. It's not taking over my life or anything, I promise. I hope that's the kind of information you were looking for? I just don't want to make it sound worse than it really is.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself doing anything repeatedly—like checking mirrors, adjusting your appearance, or seeking reassurance—to try to cope with these thoughts about your face?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I wouldn't say I do anything obsessively. I really don't want you to think I'm just standing around staring at myself all day! I know I have a job to do. \n\nBut, um... most mornings, I suppose I do end up checking my face from a few different angles in that new mirror. Just to make sure everything looks okay. And I might put on just a little extra makeup before I leave for the courthouse, to try and smooth out the unevenness. I just want to look professional for the firm, you know? \n\nAnd... well, like I mentioned before, I do try to avoid being in casual pictures if I’m not feeling okay about how my jawline looks that day. Oh, anxious? Um... well, I wouldn't use the word anxious. That sounds so... intense, you know? I really don't feel like I'm sitting there panicking or anything. 

I mean, maybe... occasionally, if I'm talking to someone new at a community event, I might just have a passing thought about whether they're noticing my skin or the little lines around my eyes. But it's just a tiny, fleeting thing! I just smile and keep the conversation going, and it's perfectly fine. I don't let it ruin the interaction at all. 

I really try to focus on being polite and making the other person feel comfortable. I'm very good at keeping things professional and pleasant. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, goodness, no! "Trouble functioning normally" sounds so... severe. I promise I haven't withdrawn from people completely or anything like that. I still get all my work done, and the attorneys are very happy with me. I would never let things fall apart. 

I mean... I suppose, if I'm being completely honest... sometimes I do spend a little extra time getting ready in the mornings. Just to make sure everything looks as neat and presentable as possible, you know? And, well, I guess that extra time has made me have to rush into the courthouse a few mornings. And... I might have missed the first few minutes of my yoga class more than once because I was running a bit behind. But I always get there! 

And I guess... well, at book club and those community events I mentioned, I have skipped being in a few of the casual photos lately. Just because I didn't want to worry about how my face would look in them. It’s just easier to offer to be the one taking the picture, right? 

But I’m definitely not withdrawing completely or letting it ruin my work! I still show up for everything and participate. I hope that’s okay? I just really want you to know I'm still handling my responsibilities well and functioning just fine. Did I explain that alright?

Like if I'm talking to one of the attorneys at work, or if I'm at the grocery store, I might wonder if the lighting is hitting my face a certain way, or if they're noticing the unevenness. I wouldn't say I do anything obsessively. I really don't want you to think I'm just standing around staring at myself all day! I just want to look professional for the firm, you know? \n\nAnd... well, like I mentioned before, I do try to avoid being in casual pictures if I’m not feeling okay about how my jawline looks that day. And sometimes... I feel a little embarrassed even admitting this, but sometimes I catch myself comparing my face to the other women I see at work. I really hope I'm not making it sound like I'm completely fixated on it, because I promise I'm not. I hope that answers your question alright?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there have been times when you felt really upset, anxious, or frustrated specifically about how your face looks, and how long do those feelings usually last?\n\nPatient: Oh, upset? Um... I really wouldn't use words like that. I don't get really upset or anxious about it, I promise. I mean, I'm a grown woman, and I know there are much bigger problems in the world than a few wrinkles or some sagging skin, right? \n\nI guess... well, maybe there are fleeting moments where I feel a tiny bit frustrated if my makeup just isn't sitting right, or if the lighting in the office bathroom is particularly harsh. But it's just a passing thought, really. It doesn't last long at all. I just kind of sigh, fix my foundation a bit, and get right back to work. \n\nI definitely don't let it ruin my day or anything like that. I'm usually very positive! I hope that's what you were asking? I just really want to be clear that I'm not falling apart over this or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you avoided social situations, events, or even certain people because you were particularly worried about how your face looked?\n\nPatient: Oh, avoid things? No, no, I wouldn't say I avoid people or events. I'm very reliable! I still get all my work done at the firm, and I make sure I'm there when people need me. I don't want you to think I'm just hiding away in my house or anything. \n\nI mean, I guess... well, there have been a few mornings where I spent a little extra time getting ready, just trying to make sure my makeup was sitting right, and maybe I had to rush into the courthouse a bit fast. I just really want to assure you that I'm keeping up with my life just fine.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there have been days when you felt so concerned about how your face looks that it was hard to focus on anything else, or that it really interfered with your ability to do your job or enjoy your day?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. And sometimes... I feel a little embarrassed even admitting this, but sometimes I catch myself comparing my face to the other women I see at work. Just looking to see if their skin is doing the same thing mine is. \n\nBut I think most women my age probably do that a little bit, don't they? I really hope I'm not making it sound like I'm completely fixated on it, because I promise I'm not. I hope that answers your question alright?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there have been times when you felt really upset, anxious, or frustrated specifically about how your face looks, and how long do those feelings usually last?\n\nPatient: Oh, upset? Um... I really wouldn't use words like that. I don't get really upset or anxious about it, I promise. I mean, I'm a grown woman, and I know there are much bigger problems in the world than a few wrinkles or some sagging skin, right? \n\nI guess... well, maybe there are fleeting moments where I feel a tiny bit frustrated if my makeup just isn't sitting right, or if the lighting in the office bathroom is particularly harsh. But it's just a passing thought, really. It doesn't last long at all. I just kind of sigh, fix my foundation a bit, and get right back to work. \n\nI definitely don't let it ruin my day or anything like that. I'm usually very positive! I hope that's what you were asking? I just really want to be clear that I'm not falling apart over this or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you avoided social situations, events, or even certain people because you were particularly worried about how your face looked?\n\nPatient: Oh, avoid things? No, no, I wouldn't say I avoid people or events. I'm very reliable! I still get all my work done at the firm, and I make sure I'm there when people need me. I don't want you to think I'm just hiding away in my house or anything. \n\nI mean, I guess... well, there have been a few mornings where I spent a little extra time getting ready, just trying to make sure my makeup was sitting right, and maybe I had to rush into the courthouse a bit fast. And... I suppose I've missed the very beginning of my yoga class a couple of times because of that, too. But I always show up! \n\nAnd, um, like I mentioned before about the pictures... I have skipped being in a few casual group photos at my book club, or at some community events. Just because... well, I didn't want to spend the whole time worrying about how my face was going to look in them. It's easier just to step out of the frame, you know? But I'm still at the events, participating and having a good time. I hope that makes sense? I just really want to assure you that I'm keeping up with my life just fine.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there have been days when you felt so concerned about how your face looks that it was hard to focus on anything else, or that it really interfered with your ability to do your job or enjoy your day?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I really wouldn't say that at all. I mean... I'm a paralegal, so my job requires a lot of attention to detail, and I would never let something like this get in the way of my work. The attorneys rely on me too much, you know? \n\nSo, no, it definitely doesn't make it hard to focus or interfere with my job. I promise I'm very dedicated to the firm. And as for enjoying my day... well, I still have a lovely time! Like I said, I still go to my yoga classes and my book club. I might have a... a brief moment where I wish my skin looked a bit firmer or more even, but I just push it right out of my mind and get back to what I'm doing. \n\nI really wouldn't let it consume my thoughts or ruin my day. I'm a very grounded person. I hope that reassures you? I just really don't want you to think I'm sitting at my desk obsessing over my face when I should be working. Does that answer what you were looking for?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there have been times when your concern about your facial appearance made you feel so upset that you had trouble functioning normally, like difficulty concentrating at work or withdrawing from people completely?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no! \"Trouble functioning normally\" sounds so... severe. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... no, I really can't think of anything like that. "Noticeable emotional distress" sounds just so... so heavy, you know? Like crying or not being able to get out of bed, and I promise I've never had anything like that happen. 

My relationships are wonderful. The ladies at book club are lovely, and the attorneys I work for are always telling me how reliable I am. I would never let a silly worry about my face get in the way of my job or my friendships. I mean, sure, I might get a tiny bit frustrated if my makeup isn't sitting right in the morning, but it's just a normal, everyday frustration. It definitely doesn't make it hard to function! 

I'm always very careful to make sure I'm doing my part and being a good friend and a good employee. I really pride myself on being dependable, no matter what. Does that make sense? I hope I'm giving you the right kind of answers... I really just want to be helpful!

I’ve also skipped a few casual photos at book club and community events because I didn’t want to worry about how my face would look.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh... well... "intense" is a bit much, I think. I really do always get my work done! I make sure of that. But... I suppose, if I'm being completely honest... maybe just briefly? 

Like, because of the extra time I spend getting ready, there have been a few mornings where I had to rush into the courthouse. Just a little rushed, I still got right to my desk! And, um, I have missed the very start of my yoga class more than once. Just the warm-up part at the beginning, though. 

And I guess... well, I have skipped a few of those casual photos at book club and community events. So I just step out of the frame for a minute. 

That's not too terrible, right? I'm still there, engaging with everyone and doing my best. I hope that's the kind of answer you were looking for... I just want to make sure I'm doing this right.

Does that answer what you were looking for?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there have been times when your concern about your facial appearance made you feel so upset that you had trouble functioning normally, like difficulty concentrating at work or withdrawing from people completely?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no! \"Trouble functioning normally\" sounds so... severe. Just because I didn't want to worry about how my face would look in them. And... I suppose I've missed the very beginning of my yoga class a couple of times because of that, too. But I always show up! \n\nAnd, um, like I mentioned before about the pictures... I have skipped being in a few casual group photos at my book club, or at some community events. Just because... well, I didn't want to spend the whole time worrying about how my face was going to look in them. It's easier just to step out of the frame, you know? But I'm still at the events, participating and having a good time. I hope that makes sense? I just really want to assure you that I'm keeping up with my life just fine.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there have been days when you felt so concerned about how your face looks that it was hard to focus on anything else, or that it really interfered with your ability to do your job or enjoy your day?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I really wouldn't say that at all. I mean... I'm a paralegal, so my job requires a lot of attention to detail, and I would never let something like this get in the way of my work. The attorneys rely on me too much, you know? \n\nSo, no, it definitely doesn't make it hard to focus or interfere with my job. I promise I'm very dedicated to the firm. And as for enjoying my day... well, I still have a lovely time! Like I said, I still go to my yoga classes and my book club. I might have a... a brief moment where I wish my skin looked a bit firmer or more even, but I just push it right out of my mind and get back to what I'm doing. \n\nI really wouldn't let it consume my thoughts or ruin my day. I'm a very grounded person. I hope that reassures you? I just really don't want you to think I'm sitting at my desk obsessing over my face when I should be working. Does that answer your question okay?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, sad, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even outside of concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Oh, sad? No, no, I wouldn't say that. I really don't feel down or depressed at all. I'm a very upbeat person, honestly! \n\nI still love doing all the things I usually do. My book club is wonderful, and yoga always makes me feel so refreshed. I definitely haven't lost interest in any of that. I mean, life is busy, of course, and sometimes I'm just a little tired after a long week at the firm, but that's just normal, right? \n\nI really try to look on the bright side of things. I wouldn't want you to think I'm sitting around feeling sorry for myself or anything like that. I'm very happy with my life, I promise. Is that... is that the kind of thing you were asking about? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you ever feel anxious or self-conscious in social situations because of how your face looks, even when you're not actively looking at yourself?\n\nPatient: Oh, anxious? Um... well, I wouldn't use the word anxious. That sounds so... intense, you know? I really don't feel like I'm sitting there panicking or anything. \n\nI mean, maybe... occasionally, if I'm talking to someone new at a community event, I might just have a passing thought about whether they're noticing my skin or the little lines around my eyes. But it's just a tiny, fleeting thing! I just smile and keep the conversation going, and it's perfectly fine. I don't let it ruin the interaction at all. \n\nI really try to focus on being polite and making the other person feel comfortable. I'm very good at keeping things professional and pleasant. I definitely wouldn't say I'm noticeably self-conscious or awkward. I hope that makes sense? I just want to be clear that I handle social situations very well. Does that answer what you were looking for?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there have been times when your concern about your facial appearance made you feel so upset that you had trouble functioning normally, like difficulty concentrating at work or withdrawing from people completely?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no! \"Trouble functioning normally\" sounds so... severe. I promise I haven't withdrawn from people completely or anything like that. I still get all my work done, and the attorneys are very happy with me. I would never let things fall apart. \n\nI mean... But I always get there! \n\nAnd I guess... well, at book club and those community events I mentioned, I have skipped being in a few of the casual photos lately. Just because I didn't want to worry about how my face would look in them. It’s just easier to offer to be the one taking the picture, right? \n\nBut I’m definitely not withdrawing completely or letting it ruin my work! I still show up for everything and participate. I hope that’s okay? I just really want you to know I'm still handling my responsibilities well and functioning just fine. Did I explain that alright?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if your concerns about your facial appearance have ever caused you to spend so much time on checking or fixing your appearance that it significantly interfered with your daily routine or responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Oh, \"significantly interfered\" is a strong way to put it. I wouldn't say it interferes, really. I always get everything done! \n\nI mean, I did mention that I take a little extra time in the mornings, right? Most days, before I leave for the courthouse, I just... you know, I check my face from a few different angles in the mirror. Just to be sure. Seeing how their skin looks compared to mine. But it's nothing major, I promise! I hope I'm not making it sound worse than it is, because I really do keep up with all my responsibilities!\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a time when your concern about your facial appearance caused you noticeable emotional distress or made it hard for you to function in any important area of life, like work, socializing, or personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, I really can't think of anything like that. \"Noticeable emotional distress\" sounds just so... so heavy, you know?     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... well, I wouldn't say a *lot* of time. I know some people can just obsess over mirrors all day, and I'm definitely not like that! I'm way too busy at the firm for that kind of thing. 

But... I guess, like I was saying about being a little rushed in the mornings? Most days, before I leave for the courthouse, I do end up checking my face from a few different angles in the bathroom mirror. Just to make sure everything looks presentable! And I usually put on a little extra makeup, just to smooth things out. It just helps me feel a bit more put-together for the day. 

And, um... sometimes at work, I might catch myself comparing my face to some of the female attorneys or the other paralegals. Just passing thoughts, really! And I already mentioned avoiding those casual pictures at book club if I'm not feeling completely okay about how I look that day. 

But it's just normal grooming and trying to put my best foot forward, you know? I'm not doing anything extreme. Does that sound like what you were asking about? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right kind of examples.
